{"parentUuid":"dbcb9fee-49a6-48fd-8d93-05805507daef","isSidechain":true,"agentId":"a35f1a5a89ceb1af1","message":{"model":"claude-opus-5","id":"msg_011CdWHj7gevx6TxSXFVLzSv","type":"message","role":"assistant","content":[{"type":"text","text":"## T4 findings — cloud service infrastructure, CORS, cache, shutdown\n\nBaseline: `bun test src` → 138 pass / 0 fail (unmodified).\n\n---\n\n### 1. `/usage` caches a transient upstream failure as \"license invalid\" — the exact thing `auth.ts` was hardened against\n\n**trace** `GET /usage` → `usageRoute` (`routes/usage.ts:93`) → `validateLicenseAndGetCredits` (`:26`) → `POST {NEXTJS}/api/license/validate` → `cacheLicense(key, {isValid:false, credits:0})` (`:48`) → Redis `license:<key>` (1 h TTL, `constants.ts:7`) → later `POST /transcribe` → `validateAuth` (`middleware/auth.ts:96`) → `getCachedLicense` cache HIT (`:110`) → `!cached.isValid` → `invalidLicenseResponse()` 401.\n\n**seam** `routes/usage.ts:validateLicenseAndGetCredits` ↔ `middleware/auth.ts:validateLicenseViaApi`. These are two independent implementations of the same operation writing the **same Redis key**. `auth.ts:70-73` carries an explicit, commented invariant:\n\n```ts\nif (response.status >= 500) {\n  console.warn(`[License] Transient ${response.status} response for ${maskedKey}; not caching`);\n  return { isValid: false, credits: 0 };\n}\n```\n\n`usage.ts` never inspects `response.ok` or `response.status` at all — it does `await response.json().catch(() => ({}))`, derives `isValid = data.valid === true`, and unconditionally writes the result to the shared cache. The hardening in `auth.ts` has an unaware sibling call site.\n\n**trigger_sketch** Vercel returns 500 from `app/api/license/validate/route.ts` (its own catch-all, `{ valid:false, error:\"Failed to validate license...\" }`, status 500 — reachable on any DB blip / cold-start timeout). A single `GET /usage?account_key=<valid-key>` during that window writes `license:<key> = {isValid:false, credits:0}` with a 3600 s TTL.\n\n**consequence** A paying user is locked out of `/transcribe`, `/post-process`, `/assistant` and `/ws/streaming-deepgram` with 401 \"Invalid license\" for up to a full hour, from **every one of the 17 prod machines** (`fly.prod.toml:21`, shared Upstash). The upstream outage may last 2 seconds. Nothing invalidates the entry except the user happening to hit `/usage?force_refresh=true` (which re-validates via `:111-126`).\n\n**confidence** high\n\n---\n\n### 2. A 429 from the license API's rate limiter is cached as a definitive \"invalid license\" — and the limiter buckets all users of a machine into one IP\n\n**trace** `usageRoute` / `validateAuth` → `POST /api/license/validate` → `licenseValidateRateLimiter.limit(getClientIPFromHeaders(req.headers))` (`nextjs/app/api/license/validate/route.ts`, limiter at `nextjs/lib/rate-limit.ts:27`, **30 req/min sliding window**) → `429 {valid:false, error:\"Too many requests...\"}` → back on Fly: `data.valid === true` is false → `isValid=false` → `cacheLicense(..., isValid:false)` (1 h).\n\n**seam** Two seams stack here:\n\n- `nextjs/server/api/routers/download-ip.ts:getClientIPFromHeaders` ↔ the Fly→Vercel hop. The Fly machine makes a plain server-to-server `fetch` with no forwarding headers, so Vercel sees the **Fly machine's egress IP** for every cloud-originated validation. The limiter's own comment (\"many users may share a NAT IP\") assumes end-user IPs; in this path *all* users behind a machine share one 30/min bucket.\n- `middleware/auth.ts:63-73` ↔ that same limiter. The comment says \"A 4xx, by contrast, is a definitive verdict from the licensing API (revoked/not-found/malformed key → valid:false); those MUST be cached.\" 429 is a 4xx and is emphatically **not** a verdict about the license. `usage.ts` has no status check at all, so it poisons too.\n\n**trigger_sketch** 31 cache-miss validations in one minute from one Fly machine — trivially reached after a Redis blip (see #3), after a deploy that cold-starts 17 machines, or driven deliberately: `/usage` has **no rate limiting of its own** and `force_refresh=true` forces a fresh upstream POST on every call. With `origin:'*'` (`index.ts:21`) any web page can drive this from visitors' browsers.\n\n**consequence** Every user whose license entry misses cache during the 429 window gets `{isValid:false}` written for an hour → mass 401 lockout of paying customers, persisting long after the burst. The 429 itself is what created the lockout, so retries deepen it.\n\n**confidence** high\n\n---\n\n### 3. Redis outage cascade: fail-open reads → thundering herd → rate-limit → mass poisoning when Redis returns\n\n**trace** Redis down → `getCachedLicense` catch → `null` (`lib/redis.ts:64-67`) → every request takes the upstream path → limiter trips at 30/min/machine → 429 → `invalidLicenseResponse()`. Simultaneously `isIPBlocked` catch → `false` (`lib/redis.ts:39-41`) → IP blocking silently off on all five routes that call it (`transcribe.ts:276`, `post-process.ts:64`, `assistant.ts:247`, `ws-streaming-deepgram.ts:138`, `usage.ts:96`). Then Redis **recovers** while the upstream is still 429-ing → `cacheLicense` starts succeeding → the 429s now get written as `isValid:false` for an hour.\n\n**seam** `lib/redis.ts` (fails open, per-call) ↔ `middleware/auth.ts` / `routes/usage.ts` (treat a cache miss as \"ask upstream\", with no circuit breaker and no bulkhead on upstream concurrency). Each layer's individual choice is defensible; the composition converts a brief Redis blip into an hour-long licensing outage that outlives the blip.\n\n**trigger_sketch** Upstash 60-second incident, or `UPSTASH_REDIS_URL`/`TOKEN` missing on one machine (`getRedis()` throws at `redis.ts:17` and every caller swallows it — the machine boots healthy and serves `/health` 200).\n\n**consequence** Fleet-wide 401s during the outage; then a *second*, longer outage caused by the recovery. Also, for the outage duration, `ip_blocked` enforcement is entirely absent with no signal — the only log is inside `getCachedLicense`; `isIPBlocked`'s catch is completely silent.\n\n**confidence** medium-high\n\n---\n\n### 4. `drainPendingDeductions` reports success it did not achieve, and its snapshot misses everything that starts after SIGTERM\n\n**trace** SIGTERM (`index.ts:102`) → `gracefulShutdown` → `drainPendingDeductions(4000)` (`middleware/credits.ts:106`) → `Promise.race([allSettled, timeout])` → `return pendingCount` → `index.ts:96` logs `machine.shutdown_drained_deductions {count}` → `process.exit(0)` (`:99`) unconditionally.\n\n**seam** Three distinct breaks between `index.ts:gracefulShutdown` and `credits.ts:drainPendingDeductions`:\n\n- **Return-value contract.** `drainPendingDeductions` returns `pendingCount` — the count *at entry* — whether the race was won by `allSettled` or by the timeout. `index.ts` reads it as \"drained\", so the operator log says `shutdown_drained_deductions: 12` on the run where all 12 were killed mid-flight. The one observable that would tell you charges are being dropped affirmatively lies.\n- **Unit/budget mismatch.** `recordLicenseUsage` (`credits.ts:71`) uses `AbortSignal.timeout(LICENSE_API_TIMEOUT_MS)` = **10 000 ms**. The drain budget is **4 000 ms**. Any deduction whose upstream call is slower than 4 s is *guaranteed* to be abandoned — and slow-upstream is precisely the condition under which deductions pile up. `SHUTDOWN_DRAIN_MS` was picked against Fly's 5 s `kill_timeout` (none of `fly.dev/staging/prod.toml` sets `kill_timeout`, so it's the 5 s default), never against the deduction's own timeout.\n- **Snapshot vs. still-serving.** `gracefulShutdown` never stops the server or waits for in-flight HTTP requests; `process.exit(0)` fires ≤4 s later. `[...inFlightDeductions]` (`credits.ts:112`) is taken once at t=0. A `/transcribe` request that was mid-transcription at SIGTERM and calls `deductCredits` at t+1 s registers into the Set *after* the snapshot and is killed without ever being awaited.\n\nAnd nothing retries: `recordLicenseUsage:74-82` logs a `console.warn` on non-ok and returns; the network catch at `:92` does the same. There is no dead-letter queue and no reconciliation job anywhere in `hyperwhisper-cloud/src`.\n\n**trigger_sketch** Any deploy or scale-down of the 17-machine fleet while transcriptions are completing. Sharpest form: upstream `/api/license/credits` is responding in 6 s (normal Vercel cold start) → 100% of in-flight charges dropped, logged as drained.\n\n**consequence** Silent revenue loss — the user keeps the transcript, the provider bill was incurred, the credit is never deducted. Repeatable on demand by a client that times its requests against deploys.\n\n**confidence** high\n\n---\n\n### 5. `app.onError` is bypassed by every `errorResponse(...)` return — raw provider bodies reach the client\n\n**trace** `transcribe.ts:672` `return errorResponse(502, ..., lastError?.message ?? ..., { requestId, provider })` and `post-process.ts:275` `return errorResponse(500, 'Post-processing failed', extractError.message, ...)`.\n\n**seam** `index.ts:67 app.onError` ↔ `lib/responses.ts:errorResponse`. `onError` only sees *thrown* errors; both of these **return** a `Response`, so the scrubber never runs. The comment at `index.ts:64-66` states the invariant precisely — \"never echo raw `err.message` to the client (it can contain env-var names, upstream provider bodies, request IDs)\" — and then `errorResponse` puts `err.message` in `message` for the client.\n\nWalk what lands in `message` for the GCS path: `gcs-storage.ts:158` throws `GCS upload failed (status=${status}, elapsedMs=…): ${preview.slice(0,400)}` where `preview` is Google's raw error body, and `:137` throws `ProviderUnavailableError('GCS upload', 'network error: ' + err.message)` whose `.message` is `` `${provider} unavailable: ${reason}` `` (`providers/types.ts:84`). A GCS 403 body names the **service-account email and the bucket name**; a DNS/TLS failure message names internal hostnames.\n\n**trigger_sketch** Single-provider chain (`isSelfOnlyChain`, `transcribe.ts:661`) with `X-STT-Provider: google` while the GCS bucket IAM is misconfigured or the bucket name is wrong → client receives 502 with 400 characters of Google's error body. `post-process.ts:275` is the more general form: any exception from the extract step is echoed verbatim.\n\n**consequence** Provider/infra detail disclosure to any caller, cross-origin-readable because of `origin:'*'`. Note `post-process.ts:275` also *deducts credits first* (`:256`) and then returns a 500 — user is billed and gets the raw error.\n\n**confidence** high\n\n---\n\n### 6. `origin:'*'` + credentials-in-query makes `/usage` a distributed license-key oracle that per-IP blocking cannot see\n\n**trace** `index.ts:20` `cors({origin:'*'})` → `usageRoute` → `getClientIP(c)` (`request-id.ts:53`) → `isIPBlocked` → key `ip_blocked:<victim-browser-IP>`.\n\n**seam** `index.ts:20` (CORS policy) ↔ `usage.ts:96` (per-IP abuse control). The IP check assumes the caller's IP is the *attacker's* IP. With `origin:'*'` and the license key carried in the **query string** rather than a cookie, a malicious page issues `fetch('https://…/usage?account_key=GUESS')` — a CORS-simple GET, no preflight — and can **read the JSON response**, so it gets a clean valid/invalid oracle. Each probe originates from a different visitor's IP, so `ip_blocked` buckets fill one probe at a time and never trip. `allowHeaders` gates nothing here: it only constrains preflighted requests, and this attack is preflight-free. `allowMethods: ['GET','POST','OPTIONS']` likewise permits exactly the two methods that spend credits.\n\nSame shape applies to `/assistant`: it is multipart, and `multipart/form-data` is a CORS-simple content type, so a cross-origin `POST /assistant?account_key=…` also skips preflight entirely.\n\n**trigger_sketch** Any site with modest traffic embeds a loop over candidate keys; a 401 body means invalid, a 200 body means valid and leaks `credits_remaining`. As a bonus this drives the upstream `/api/license/validate` rate limiter (finding #2) from the Fly egress IP.\n\n**consequence** License-key brute force / enumeration with attribution washed through third-party browsers, plus a free amplifier onto the licensing API, plus balance disclosure for any key held by a compromised page (e.g. one that scraped the key from a URL in a `Referer` header or a shared support log).\n\n**confidence** medium-high\n\n---\n\n### 7. `getClientIP` collapses to the literal string `'unknown'`, which is a valid Redis key\n\n**trace** `request-id.ts:53-60` → returns `'unknown'` when the peer is 6PN **or** when neither `Fly-Client-IP` nor `X-Forwarded-For` is present → `isIPBlocked('unknown')` → `ip_blocked:unknown`.\n\n**seam** `request-id.ts:getClientIP` (returns a sentinel) ↔ `redis.ts:isIPBlocked` (treats every string as an address). The sentinel is a shared bucket, not an address. An operator who sees `'unknown'` abuse in logs and runs `SET ip_blocked:unknown true` instantly 403s every internal/6PN caller and every request that arrives without Fly headers — including anything reaching the machine during a Fly proxy-header regression. Symmetrically, all off-edge callers share one block entry, so blocking one blocks all and blocking none blocks none.\n\n**trigger_sketch** Manual ops action (there is **no writer** of `ip_blocked:*` anywhere in `hyperwhisper-cloud/src` — the block list is populated by hand, so a typo'd or sentinel key is the realistic path).\n\n**consequence** Fleet-wide 403 on `/usage`, `/transcribe`, `/post-process`, `/assistant`, `/ws/streaming-deepgram` from one `redis-cli` line, with no code path that can undo it.\n\n**confidence** medium\n\n---\n\n### 8. `/usage` trims only one of its three key aliases; the upstream trims all three → split cache entries for one account\n\n**trace** `usage.ts:102-103`:\n```ts\nconst licenseKey =\n  c.req.query('account_key') || c.req.query('license_key') || c.req.query('identifier')?.trim() || null;\n```\n→ `cacheLicense(licenseKey, …)` → `license:<untrimmed>`. Meanwhile `nextjs/app/api/license/credits/route.ts` does `findAccountByKey(licenseKey.trim())` and `checkLicenseKey` on the validate side both normalize.\n\n**seam** `usage.ts:103` ↔ `redis.ts:70 cacheLicense` ↔ the Next.js trim. `.trim()` binds only to the third operand of the `||` chain — `account_key` and `license_key` pass through raw. `transcribe.ts:396` uses `rawQuery(...)` with **no trim on any alias**. So `KEY`, `KEY%0A` and `%20KEY` are one account upstream but three independent 1-hour cache entries downstream.\n\n**trigger_sketch** A key pasted with a trailing newline into the desktop app's settings field. The app sends `identifier=KEY%0A` to `/usage` (trimmed → caches under `license:KEY`) and `license_key=KEY%0A` to `/transcribe` (untrimmed → caches under `license:KEY\\n`). Both work upstream, so nothing looks broken.\n\n**consequence** The balance shown in the app (read from the trimmed entry) stops tracking actual spend, because `recordLicenseUsage`'s write-back (`credits.ts:86`) lands on the untrimmed entry. User watches their credit balance sit still while credits drain, or vice versa. Also doubles upstream validate load per user, feeding #2.\n\n**confidence** medium\n\n---\n\n### 9. `/usage?force_refresh=true` renews `isValid:true` for an hour without ever re-validating the license\n\n**trace** `usage.ts:110-121`: `getCachedLicense` → `cached?.isValid` true → `getCreditsBalance` (`:60`) → `GET /api/license/credits` → on 2xx → `cacheLicense(key, {isValid: true, credits})` (`:81`, **hard-coded `true`**) → 1 h TTL renewed.\n\n**seam** `usage.ts:81` ↔ `nextjs/app/api/license/credits/route.ts`. The hard-coded `isValid:true` is *currently* justified because that GET returns 400 unless `license.status === \"granted\"` — but nothing in the Fly code expresses or enforces that dependency, and `usage.ts` re-derives validity from an endpoint whose contract is \"give me a number.\" The `POST` write-back in `credits.ts:86` does the same thing (`isValid: true` hard-coded, keyed off `credits_remaining` being a number).\n\nThe live consequence today is the **TTL renewal**: as long as a client polls `/usage?force_refresh=true` more often than hourly, the `license:<key>` entry never expires and the *validate* endpoint is never consulted again. Any state change that only `/api/license/validate` knows about (Polar-side revocation, anything `checkLicenseKey` checks beyond `status`) is invisible for as long as the client keeps polling.\n\n**trigger_sketch** Refund/chargeback flips the account; the desktop app's periodic `/usage?force_refresh=true` keeps renewing `isValid:true` from the credits endpoint's happy path.\n\n**consequence** Delayed (potentially indefinite) revocation for a still-polling client; and if the credits endpoint's status gate is ever relaxed, this silently becomes a straight authorization bypass with no code change on the Fly side.\n\n**confidence** medium (mechanism certain; blast radius depends on `checkLicenseKey`'s superset of checks, which I did not fully read)\n\n---\n\n### 10. `app.options('*')` is dead code, and `notFound` → 405 advertises endpoints that don't exist\n\n**trace** `index.ts:20` cors middleware → for `OPTIONS`, Hono's `cors()` short-circuits and returns 204 itself without calling `next()` → `app.options('*')` at `index.ts:26` is unreachable. Separately `index.ts:60 app.notFound` returns `405 \"Method not allowed\"` for unmatched **paths**.\n\n**seam** `index.ts:26` ↔ Hono's `cors()` (the handler was written assuming the middleware falls through). And `index.ts:60` ↔ any client doing feature detection: 405 semantically asserts \"this resource exists, this method doesn't,\" so `OPTIONS /does-not-exist` returns 204 with `Access-Control-Allow-Methods: GET, POST, OPTIONS` and `GET /does-not-exist` returns 405 — a probe cannot distinguish \"endpoint not in this build\" from \"wrong verb.\" With a staged rollout across 17 machines and Fly anycast, a client that probes for a newer endpoint gets 405 and, per HTTP convention, may retry with a different method rather than falling back.\n\nRelated and more concrete: `allowMethods` omits `HEAD`, and only `app.get`/`app.post` are registered — a `HEAD /health` monitor falls through to `notFound` and gets 405 rather than 200.\n\n**consequence** Misleading capability advertisement; a HEAD-based health checker reports the machine unhealthy. Low severity, but both are nameable.\n\n**confidence** low\n\n---\n\n### 11. SIGTERM orphans user voice recordings in the private GCS bucket, and the delete failure is unobservable\n\n**trace** `transcribe.ts` (Google Chirp path) → `uploadTranscriptionAudio` (`gcs-storage.ts:100`) → PUT to `stt-temp/<ts>-<uuid>.<ext>` → Speech V2 recognize → `deleteTranscriptionAudio(ref)` in a `finally`.\n\n**seam** `gcs-storage.ts` lifecycle contract (`:9-13`, \"caller invokes `deleteTranscriptionAudio(ref)` in a `finally`\") ↔ `index.ts:99 process.exit(0)`. A `finally` block is not a process-exit hook. `gracefulShutdown` drains *deductions* only — it does not track, await, or even know about in-flight GCS refs, and it does not stop the server before exiting. Enumerated orphan paths:\n\n- SIGTERM between upload and delete → `process.exit(0)` ≤4 s later, `finally` never runs.\n- Bun OOM / Fly hardware kill — no signal at all. Note `computeUploadTimeoutMs` (`:36`) grants a 2 GB payload ~20 000 s of upload budget on a machine whose audio cap is `MAX_AUDIO_SIZE_BYTES = 2 GB` (`constants.ts:10`), so the window is genuinely long.\n- Delete times out at `DELETE_TIMEOUT_MS = 10_000` (`:29`) or returns non-404 non-ok → `console.warn('gcs.delete_failed'|'gcs.delete_error')` and returns void. `deleteTranscriptionAudio` **cannot fail** from the caller's perspective — there is no return value, no counter, no metric.\n- `deleteTranscriptionAudio` calls `getGoogleAccessToken()` *again* (`:187`). If the cached token expired between upload and delete and the mint now fails, the delete throws inside the try and is swallowed.\n\n**trigger_sketch** Any deploy while a Chirp transcription is in flight. `min_machines_running = 17` with a rolling deploy makes this near-certain per release.\n\n**consequence** User voice recordings persist in the bucket indefinitely. The only backstop is a server-side lifecycle rule that the module's own comment (`:14-17`) describes as something the bucket \"SHOULD also have\" — i.e. unverified in code, and if it is absent or scoped to the wrong prefix, nothing tells you: the orphan count is not derivable from any log line, since a crash produces no `gcs.delete_*` entry at all.\n\n**confidence** medium-high\n\n---\n\n### 12. Google token `_inflight` dedup is per-machine but the failure path leaves a window; TTL is computed from a clock the token doesn't share\n\n**trace** `getGoogleAccessToken` (`google-auth.ts:125`) → Redis `google_oauth_token` miss → `_inflight = mintAndCacheToken()` (`:145`) → `jwt.authorize()` → `computeCacheTtlSeconds(expiry_date)` (`:59`) → `redis.set(..., {ex: ttlSeconds})` → `_inflight = null` (`:116`).\n\n**seam** `google-auth.ts:88/96` (`_inflight = null` inside `mintAndCacheToken` **before** throwing) ↔ `google-auth.ts:137` (`if (_inflight) return _inflight`). The failure path nulls `_inflight` and then throws — so the rejection propagates to every caller already holding that promise (correct), but callers arriving in the same tick after the null and before the throw resolves get no dedup and each start a fresh `authorize()`. Under a Google auth outage with `min_machines_running = 17` this is a per-request mint storm against Google's token endpoint, from 17 machines, with no backoff (`retryWithBackoff` in `utils.ts:63` exists but is not used here).\n\nSecond seam, on units/clocks: `computeCacheTtlSeconds` derives the Redis TTL from `expiry_date - Date.now()` — Google's absolute expiry timestamp minus the **Fly machine's local clock**. The 600 s safety margin absorbs modest skew, but a machine whose clock is >10 min fast caches a token with a TTL longer than its real life; a machine whose clock is slow under-caches. The cache is **shared across all 17 machines**, so one skewed machine's TTL governs everyone's token. The 401-recovery hook `invalidateGoogleAccessToken` (`:156`) exists but I found no call site in the GCS path — `gcs-storage.ts` never calls it, so a mid-flight token expiry during upload/delete surfaces as a plain 401 → for upload, a non-transient `Error` (`:158`, since 401 is neither 429 nor ≥500) that fails fast rather than re-minting.\n\n**trigger_sketch** Google IAM token endpoint 503s for 30 s during peak; or a single machine with NTP drift caches a token whose real expiry precedes its Redis TTL, and all 17 machines then serve GCS uploads with a dead token until the TTL lapses.\n\n**consequence** Chirp transcriptions fail with a non-retryable 502/500 (and, per finding #5, leak Google's raw 401 body to the client) for the remainder of the cached TTL — up to 50 min in the `FALLBACK_TTL_SECONDS` case.\n\n**confidence** medium (the `_inflight` window is narrow and single-tick; the shared-TTL/no-invalidation interaction is the more solid half)\n\n---\n\n##
